URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7244349446279327745

This is a famous interview question asked by Amazon:

Question: Given the reviews table, write a query to retrieve the average star rating for each product, grouped by month. The output should display the month as a numerical value, product ID, and average star rating rounded to two decimal places. Sort the output first by month and then by product ID.

You can find the full details here: https://lnkd.in/ggXuYQwG

Let's solve it together:

As a interviewee, when given this SQL problem, my approach would focus on clarity and accuracy.

The task is to calculate the average review ratings for each product by month from a reviews dataset, where we have fields like submit_date, product_id, and stars. The challenge is to group the reviews by product and month while calculating the average rating and rounding it to two decimal places.

Step 1: Extract the Month
Since there’s no dedicated month field, the first step is to extract the month from submit_date. SQL’s EXTRACT() function will allow us to do that:

Step 2: Group the Data
We need to group the data by both month and product_id to get the average rating for each combination. The GROUP BY clause is used for this purpose:

Step 3: Calculate the Average
Using AVG(), we can calculate the average rating for each group. To ensure that the results are rounded to two decimal places, I will use the ROUND() function:

Step 4: Sort the Results: 
Finally, the results are sorted by month and product_id using the ORDER BY clause.

This is a medium level problem and should take between 5-10 minutes to get to the solution.
I hope this helps you ace your next interview.

Next, follow this step-by-step strategy to learn SQL in less than 6 weeks:

𝐁𝐞𝐠𝐢𝐧𝐧𝐞𝐫 𝐋𝐞𝐯𝐞𝐥 (𝐖𝐞𝐞𝐤 𝟏-𝟐):
— Basics of SELECT, WHERE, GROUP BY
— INNER, LEFT, RIGHT Joins
— Aggregation Functions (SUM, COUNT, AVG, etc)

𝐈𝐧𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦𝐞𝐝𝐢𝐚𝐭𝐞 𝐋𝐞𝐯𝐞𝐥 (𝐖𝐞𝐞𝐤 𝟑-𝟒):
— Window Functions (ROW_NUMBER, RANK)
— CTEs and Subqueries
— CASE statements for conditional logic

𝐀𝐝𝐯𝐚𝐧𝐜𝐞𝐝 𝐋𝐞𝐯𝐞𝐥 (𝐖𝐞𝐞𝐤 𝟓-𝟔):
— SQL Performance Optimization (indexes, query tuning)
— Data Modeling
— Complex Joins & Relationships

Top Platforms to Practice SQL:
— DataLemur 🐵 (Ace the SQL & Data Interview) - https://datalemur.com/
— StrataScratch - www.stratascratch.com/

— Mode - https://mode.com/

1. Alex Freberg
2. Ankit Bansal

4. Ken Jee
5. Shashank Mishra 🇮🇳

Remember: Practice every day, even if it's just one problem!
Consistent Learning and Practice will set you apart from the competition.

I promise just 2 months of consistent hardwork will help you in finding your next role too!

Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7445742189457965056

Here are 30 Data Analyst case studies you must know before your next interview.
(Save this - interviewers love asking these)

I have conducted over 100 data analyst interviews.
The candidates who stand out are never the ones who memorized the most SQL.

They are the ones who can walk through a real business problem end to end - without freezing.

Here are the 6 categories that come up most 👇

Sales & Revenue Analysis
-- Analyze monthly revenue trends to identify growth patterns and fluctuations
-- Identify top products driving majority of total revenue
-- Break down revenue by segments and channels to uncover opportunities
-- Recommend strategies to improve declining product performance

Customer Segmentation
-- Segment customers by purchase behavior, frequency, and engagement
-- Identify high and low value users using RFM-based segmentation
-- Analyze retention trends across segments to understand long-term behavior

Churn Analysis
-- Identify users who stopped using the product within a defined period
-- Compare churned vs active users to find key behavioral differences
-- Detect early churn signals like reduced activity and declining engagement

Funnel Analysis
-- Track user journey from signup to conversion across each stage
-- Identify drop-off points where users exit or lose interest
-- Compare funnel performance across different acquisition channels

A/B Testing
-- Define a clear hypothesis before testing any product improvement
-- Split users into control and test groups for unbiased comparison
-- Analyze statistical significance to validate experiment results

Product Analytics
-- Analyze feature usage across different user segments
-- Identify most and least used features for optimization decisions
-- Track impact of new features on overall product performance

The candidates who nail these do not just describe the analysis.

They connect every step back to a business decision.

Any analyst can say "I would segment the customers."

The ones who get hired say why and what the business should do with it.

Which of these have you actually practiced end to end?

♻️ Repost to help someone preparing for a data analyst interview
💭 Tag someone interviewing for data roles right now
📩 Get my full interview prep guide: https://lnkd.in/gjUqmQ5H

How to beat 99% of the competition in the current job market:

- Go quiet for 3 months
- SET a goal to secure an X role with a Y salary 
- Stop self-comparison and FOCUS on your journey
- Seek excellence in 3-5 key skills for your target role (Possible in 3 months)
- Do a ton of Information Interviews to learn industry best practices
- Record each interview, learn from your flaws, and do better the next time.
- Lastly, Apply to roles as much as POSSIBLE (Without compromising Quality)

Most Important: Every 2 weeks; Self-evaluate, acknowledge that you are doing everything that you possibly can, be proud of your efforts, and practice GRATITUDE.

Consistency and a Laser-Focus is how you blow past everyone else, Do you agree?

This Resume landed me multiple FAANG interviews,
Steal my step-by-step strategy on how I did it below:

Your resume is your entry ticket to a job interview,
And with the right resume, you can open some great interview opportunities for yourself.

Here’s exactly how you should structure a good resume:

1. Education:
-- Let the hiring manager know the knowledge that you’ve been trained for.

2. Skills:
-- Convey the skills that you can contribute to the team.

3. Work Experience:
-- Showcase how your previous experience will help you successfully carry out the responsibilities of the role that you’re interviewing for.

4. Projects:
This section helps you portray your expertise in a broad range of skill sets.

5. Leadership and Awards:
Portraying a holistic personality further adds to the credibility of you as a candidate.

Having your resume structured this way will be the easiest way for the hiring manager to select you as a potential candidate and send out an interview invite.

In addition, here are a few tips for a fully optimized resume:

-- If you have less than 10 years of experience, always keep a 1-page resume.
-- Make sure to include tons of metrics and data to support your achievements
-- Always format your bullet like this: Accomplished [X] as measured by [Y], by doing [Z].
-- Tailor a unique resume for each job application to make it ATS-friendly.

If you are currently struggling to get callbacks, try these suggestions.
I assure you, that by implementing them for just 2-3 weeks, you’ll see an increase in the number of callbacks that you get.

--- Post 124 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 455 reactions, 31 comments, 29 reposts (Score: 2,643)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7377338564423901184

This is how to crack a $250,000 salary job:

(Resume secrets shared by a FAANG Recruiter below):

You only have ~6 seconds to get a job!

That’s the average time a recruiter spends on your resume before making a decision.

Here’s how to maximize those 6 seconds with strategies most candidates ignore 👇

1. Beat the ATS before you beat the recruiter:

70–80% of resumes get filtered out by ATS.

-- Use tools like Wisedoc AI Recruiting or Jobscan to compare your resume against the JD and identify missing keywords.
-- Don’t “keyword dump.” Place them naturally in your Work Experience & Skills sections.

2. Tailor in minutes, not hours:

Recruiters can catch generic resumes instantly.

-- Instead of rewriting from scratch, build a master resume.
-- For each application, adjust just 3 things:
A. Headline/title
B. Top 3 bullet points
C. Skills section

That alone increases relevance by 50%+.

3. Numbers > narratives. Always:

Recruiters look for impact. If your bullets don’t quantify results, they blend into white noise.

-- Bad: “Responsible for customer support.”
-- Good: “Handled 50+ support tickets/day, reducing average resolution time by 30%.”
 
If you don’t have hard numbers, use proxies: scale (“served 200+ clients”), frequency (“led weekly workshops”), or efficiency (“cut process time by half”).

4. Design for scanning, not reading:

"Recruiters skim in an “F-pattern.”

-- Place your strongest metrics in the first 2 lines of each section.
-- Use 10–12pt font, no fancy graphics, and clear section headers.
-- Stick to formats that ATS can parse (PDF/Word, no text boxes).

5. Balance skills with story:

Yes, technical skills matter, but soft skills tip the scale.
In fact, LinkedIn data shows 89% of failed hires are due to poor culture fit.

Weave them into your achievements:
“Led a 5-member cross-functional team to deliver project 3 weeks early.” → shows leadership + adaptability without buzzwords.

BONUS: You can ace your next interview by preparing using Final Round AI.
Start Preparing Here: https://lnkd.in/gsCKAg7K

Remember: A resume isn’t your life story.

It’s a marketing document designed to pass filters, prove value, and earn you the interview.

Author: sohansethi

5 things I wish I had known before coming to the US:

✅ Myth No. 1: Life in the USA is always easy and luxurious.
❌ Reality: Landing in the US is just the beginning of your struggles. 

💬 Reason: Adapting to a new culture, navigating immigration, and building a career are tougher than they appear.

✅ Myth No. 2: America is still the ultimate land of opportunities.
❌ Reality: Let alone America, currently no country is the land of opportunities.

💬 Reason: While opportunities exist, competition is fierce, especially for international students. Securing a job involves countless rejections, endless networking, and unwavering perseverance.

✅ Myth No. 3: A U.S. degree guarantees financial freedom.
❌ Reality: Education loans will become a burden you can't get easily rid of.

💬 Reason: Most students take thousands of $$$ in Education loans. They can soon become overwhelming, especially with low entry-level salaries and steep interest rates.

✅ Myth No. 4: You'll always have a vibrant social life.
❌ Reality: There will come days that will be the loneliest time of your lives. 

💬 Reason: Many international students face periods of isolation, battling anxiety, homesickness, and the pressure to succeed—all in silence.

✅ Myth No. 5: Living in the USA provides stability.
❌ Reality: Life will be very unpredictable and you'll feel lost between two worlds.

💬 Reason: Layoffs, visa issues, and constant uncertainty about the future make it difficult to feel settled, no matter how long you've been in the United States.

However, on the other side life in the US will introduce you to a diverse community, a beautiful country, meaningful experiences, and a journey of self-transformation, where you get an opportunity to build a life of freedom and prosperity.

What are your thoughts? Do you have similar experiences?

Let me know in the comments below.

Here's my Ultimate SQL Joins Cheatsheet:

(Save this post now for your interview prep)

Most candidates learn JOINs by memorizing patterns:
INNER JOIN, LEFT JOIN, RIGHT JOIN, FULL JOIN.

But interviews don’t test memory.
They test understanding.

Instead of throwing queries at you, this Cheatsheet visually shows:
• How rows from two tables actually match
• What each JOIN includes and excludes
• How exclude JOINs work step by step
• The real difference between UNION and UNION ALL
• What your final result set really looks like

If SQL is part of your interviews, daily work, or data pipelines - this is one of those fundamentals you can’t afford to miss.

Remember: Daily practice is key to acing SQL Interviews.

--- Post 134 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 421 reactions, 24 comments, 51 reposts (Score: 2,447)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7441617629993316352

Nobody is going to hire you because you watched 50 hours of tutorials.

They hire you because you can take messy data, find what matters, and explain it to someone who does not care about your process.

That only happens if you learn analytics in the right order.
Most people do not.

Here is the order that actually makes sense:
1. Data Sources → You need to know where data lives before you touch it. Databases, APIs, CSVs, cloud storage, data warehouses. This is the starting line.

2. Data Collection → Getting the data is half the battle. Handling missing values, duplicate records, type conversions, outliers — this starts here, not later.

3. Data Cleaning → The step nobody talks about enough. 80% of a real analyst's job is right here. If your data is dirty, your insights are wrong. Period.

4. Data Transformation → Now you shape it. Aggregation, normalization, feature engineering, merging tables. This is where raw data becomes something you can actually analyze.

5. Exploratory Data Analysis → Before you build anything, you explore. Descriptive stats, correlation, trend spotting, hypothesis testing. You are asking questions, not answering them yet.

6. Data Visualization → Now you make it visible. Charts, dashboards, Tableau, Power BI, Python libraries. The goal is clarity, not decoration.

7. Business Analytics → This is where the real value shows up. KPIs, customer behavior, revenue insights, market segmentation. This is what stakeholders actually care about.

8. Data Storytelling → The most underrated skill in analytics. You can have perfect charts and still lose the room. Insight communication, narrative, business recommendations — this is what separates good analysts from great ones.

9. Analytics Tools & Technologies → Excel, SQL, Python (Pandas, NumPy), Tableau, Power BI, cloud platforms. Learn these in parallel with steps 1 through 8, not before them.

The mistake most tutorials make:
They teach you tools first and context second.
You end up knowing how to write a SQL query but not knowing what business question you are trying to answer.
Learn the process. The tools follow.

Which step gave you the most trouble when you were learning? Drop it below.
♻️ Repost if this helps someone in your network
💭 Tag someone who is learning data analytics right now

--- Post 140 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 339 reactions, 55 comments, 58 reposts (Score: 2,361)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7450184400560103425

Here's my Ultimate Advanced Python Tricks Cheatsheet for Data Analysts:
(Save this - these are the ones that actually matter in real work)

Every analyst knows pd.read_csv() and df.head().
The ones getting promoted know what comes after that.

Here are 15 advanced Python tricks that separate junior analysts from senior ones 👇

1. Memory-Optimized Data Loading
Specify data types while loading to reduce memory and speed up processing.

2. Select Columns Efficiently
Always load only the columns you need — never the entire dataset.

3. Conditional Filtering with Multiple Rules
Apply complex business logic to slice data precisely in one line.

4. Vectorized Feature Engineering
Multiply columns directly instead of loops — faster and more scalable.

5. Use query() for Cleaner Filtering
Write SQL-like filter conditions that are readable and easy to maintain.

6. Advanced GroupBy with Multiple Aggregations
Generate sum, mean, and max insights across categories in one operation.

7. Window Functions SQL Style
Rank rows within groups directly in Python — exactly like SQL window functions.

8. Rolling Window Analysis
Calculate 7-day moving averages to smooth trends for time-series reporting.

9. Handle Missing Data Strategically
Fill nulls with the median — preserves distribution instead of distorting it.

10. Efficient Deduplication with Priority
Sort by date first then drop duplicates — keeps the most recent record per user.

11. Merge Datasets Like SQL Joins
Combine two dataframes on a key column exactly like a SQL LEFT JOIN.

12. Pivot Tables for Quick Reporting
Summarize revenue by category and region instantly without building a dashboard.

13. Explode Nested Data
Transform list-like columns into individual rows for deeper granular analysis.

14. Apply Custom Functions Efficiently
Use np.where for conditional logic - significantly faster than apply() on large datasets.

15. Chain Operations for Clean Pipelines
Drop nulls, filter, and engineer features in one readable chained expression.

Most analysts use Python like a calculator.
Senior analysts use it like a pipeline.
The difference is not knowing more functions.

It is knowing how to chain them together to go from raw messy data to a clean business insight in minutes.

Save this. Practice each one on a real dataset.
Watching is not learning. Building is.
Which of these are you not using yet?

♻️ Repost to help someone level up their Python skills
💭 Tag a data analyst who needs to see this
📩 Get my full Python analytics guide: https://lnkd.in/gjUqmQ5H

--- Post 144 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 328 reactions, 59 comments, 41 reposts (Score: 2,312)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7448013102719090688

A model is only as good as the pipeline behind it.
Most analysts build great models on broken pipelines.
Then wonder why results don't hold in production.

Here's what mastery actually looks like - 8 pipelines, fully mapped:

1. Regression - For predicting continuous values.
Starts with collection and cleaning. Ends with hyperparameter tuning and prediction output.

2. Classification - For labeled outcomes.
Class imbalance handling and threshold tuning are where most people cut corners. Don't.

3. Clustering - For unlabeled data.
PCA for dimensionality reduction. Silhouette score for validation. Skipping validation is the most common mistake.

4. Time Series - Trend analysis, seasonality, lag features, rolling statistics.
ARIMA and Prophet for training. Backtesting before any forecast goes live.

5. Recommendation Systems - User-item matrix.
Cosine and Pearson similarity. Personalization and ranking layer before output. Netflix and Spotify live here.

6. Anomaly Detection - Pattern modeling.
Isolation Forest for ML-based detection. Threshold setting determines your false positive rate. Get it wrong and alerts become noise.

7. NLP - Text cleaning, tokenization, TF-IDF, word embeddings, model training, F1 evaluation.
The pipeline most analysts underestimate most.

8. Feature Engineering - The pipeline that makes every other pipeline better.
Imputation, encoding, feature creation, scaling, selection. Garbage in - garbage out. Always.

Eight pipelines. Each one a skill on its own.

Master all eight and you're not just an analyst.
You're a full ML practitioner.

Which pipeline is your weakest? Drop it below 👇

============================================================

--- Post 147 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 389 reactions, 25 comments, 13 reposts (Score: 2,221)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7387492435205152768

This Resume helped me get 40+ interviews:
Including interviews from Google, Amazon, JPMorganChase, Capital One, CVS Health and many more!

Here's how you can get them too:

Your resume is your entry ticket to a job interview,
And with the right resume, you can open some great interview opportunities for yourself.

Here’s exactly how you should structure a good resume:

1. Education:
-- Let the hiring manager know the knowledge that you’ve been trained for.

2. Skills:
-- Convey the skills that you can contribute to the team.

3. Work Experience:
-- Showcase how your previous experience will help you successfully carry out the responsibilities of the role that you’re interviewing for.

4. Projects:
This section helps you portray your expertise in a broad range of skill sets.

5. Leadership and Awards:
Portraying a holistic personality further adds to the credibility of you as a candidate.

Having your resume structured this way will be the easiest way for the hiring manager to select you as a potential candidate and send out an interview invite.

In addition, here are a few tips for a fully optimized resume:

-- If you have less than 10 years of experience, always keep a 1-page resume.
-- Make sure to include tons of metrics and data to support your achievements
-- Always format your bullet like this: Accomplished [X] as measured by [Y], by doing [Z].
-- Tailor a unique resume for each job application to make it ATS-friendly.

If you are currently struggling to get callbacks, try these suggestions.
I assure you, that by implementing them for just 2-3 weeks, you’ll see an increase in the number of callbacks that you get.

My aim with this post is to help everyone currently in the job market to excel in their job search process.

Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7442326658499461120

Ultimate Basic SQL vs Advanced SQL Cheatsheet

(Save this post - this is exactly what gets you promoted)

Basic SQL gets you hired.
Advanced SQL gets you promoted.

Most analysts stop learning the moment they can pull data without errors.
That is exactly where they get stuck.

I’ve managed data teams long enough to see this pattern clearly.

The analysts who grow are not the ones who know the most queries.
They are the ones who think differently about what SQL is actually for.

Here is the full breakdown 👇

Goal
-- Basic: Get a correct result. If the query runs, the job is done.
-- Advanced: Get the right result efficiently, cleanly, and reusable. Correctness is just the starting point.

Mindset
-- Basic: Reactive. Waits for a question and answers it literally.
-- Advanced: Proactive. Thinks about the real business need behind the question.

Core Skills
-- Basic: SELECT, WHERE, GROUP BY, aggregations, JOINs, LIMIT, DISTINCT
-- Advanced: Window functions (RANK, LAG, LEAD), CTEs, subqueries, CASE WHEN, optimization, execution plans

Output Type
-- Basic: Raw data. Numbers that answer the surface-level question.
-- Advanced: Insights. Clear story that drives decisions and action.

Code Style
-- Basic: One long query. Hardcoded values. Difficult to debug or reuse.
-- Advanced: Modular CTEs. Clean, readable logic. Easy to test and extend.

Performance
-- Basic: Works on small data. Breaks or slows in production.
-- Advanced: Scales to large datasets. Uses indexes and avoids expensive scans.

Problem Approach
-- Basic: Answers exactly what was asked.
-- Advanced: Questions the problem. Validates metrics and assumptions.

Collaboration
-- Basic: Written for personal use. Hard for others to understand.
-- Advanced: Written for teams. Clear, documented, and easy to review.

Error Handling
-- Basic: Breaks on NULLs, duplicates, and edge cases.
-- Advanced: Handles edge cases. Uses COALESCE, NULLIF, and proper deduplication.

Stakeholder Trust
-- Basic: Delivers numbers. Trust builds slowly over time.
-- Advanced: Delivers insights with context, clarity, and recommendations.

Reusability
-- Basic: One-off queries every time. No shared logic.
-- Advanced: Builds reusable views, templates, and shared data models.

Here is the honest truth:

Every analyst starts with Basic SQL. That is expected.

But staying there is a choice - not a limitation.

The difference between the analyst who gets promoted and the one who stays stuck is rarely technical ability.

It is whether they stop growing the moment their queries start working.

Advanced SQL is not about memorizing more syntax.

It is about changing how you think about data, business problems, and the people who depend on your work.

Save this. Share it with your team.

Which area are you still working on?

♻️ Repost to help someone level up their SQL
💭 Tag a data analyst who needs to see this
📩 Get my full SQL career guide: https://lnkd.in/gkaAKRqh

--- Post 153 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 293 reactions, 58 comments, 19 reposts (Score: 2,083)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7450540306170576896

I had no connections. No referrals. No network.
I moved to the US for my master's degree knowing nobody in the professional world.
47 LinkedIn followers. A resume nobody was reading. Zero callbacks.
I built everything from scratch.

Here is exactly how.

Just a LinkedIn profile with 47 followers and a resume nobody was reading.

Here is exactly how I built a network that led to my first role.

𝗙𝗶𝘅 𝘆𝗼𝘂𝗿 𝗟𝗶𝗻𝗸𝗲𝗱𝗜𝗻 𝗳𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁

Nobody responds to a connection request from a blank profile.

-- Spend one week getting it right before reaching out to anyone. 
-- Clear headline. 
-- Strong about section. 
-- Measurable bullet points.

𝗥𝗲𝘀𝗼𝘂𝗿𝗰𝗲: Jeff Su - youtube.com/@JeffSu

𝗨𝘀𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗮𝗹𝘂𝗺𝗻𝗶 𝗳𝗶𝗹𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗱𝗮𝗶𝗹𝘆

-- Go to your university's LinkedIn page. 
-- Search a target company. 
-- Get a list of alumni working there right now.

These are the warmest contacts you will ever find. They went to your school. They understand your journey.

I sent 5 alumni messages every morning. Not asking for a job. Asking for 15 minutes to learn about their path.

𝗧𝗿𝗲𝗮𝘁 𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘆 𝗰𝗼𝗳𝗳𝗲𝗲 𝗰𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗹𝗶𝗸𝗲 𝗮𝗻 𝗶𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗿𝘃𝗶𝗲𝘄

-- Prepare 5 specific questions. 
-- Research the person beforehand. 
-- Send a follow-up within 24 hours referencing something specific from your conversation.

3 of my coffee chats turned into referrals.
1 led to my first role.

𝗥𝗲𝘀𝗼𝘂𝗿𝗰𝗲: Andrew LaCivita - youtube.com/@andylacivita

𝗝𝗼𝗶𝗻 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗺𝘂𝗻𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗲𝘀 𝘄𝗵𝗲𝗿𝗲 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗳𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻𝗮𝗹𝘀 𝗴𝗮𝘁𝗵𝗲𝗿

-- Meetup → Local data and tech meetups. Show up consistently.
-- DataTalks.Club → Free online community for data professionals. Active Slack. International friendly.
-- LinkedIn Groups → Comment with value daily. Not just "great post."

Showing up once means nothing.
Showing up every month means everything.

𝗣𝗼𝘀𝘁 𝗰𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝗼𝗻 𝗟𝗶𝗻𝗸𝗲𝗱𝗜𝗻

Every time I posted about data analytics - a project, a resource, something I learned, someone new connected with me.

Recruiters started finding me instead of me chasing them.

6 months: 47 followers to 2,000+.

Two of my first job leads came directly from my content.

𝗕𝗲 𝘀𝗽𝗲𝗰𝗶𝗳𝗶𝗰 𝗶𝗻 𝗲𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘆 𝗼𝘂𝘁𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗰𝗵 𝗺𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗮𝗴𝗲

Every message I sent had three things:

-- A specific reason why I was reaching out to them
-- One genuine observation about their career
-- A single small ask - a conversation, not a job

Response rate went from near zero to 40%.

The network you build in your first year determines your first 5 years.

Show up every day. Be specific. Follow up every time.

Where are you in your networking journey right now?

♻️ Repost - every international student needs to read this
💭 Tag someone who just moved to the US for their studies

1. Landing in the US is just the start of your struggles. 
-- Things don't get easy, they only get tougher and complicated for the next few years.

2. Let alone America, currently no country is the land of opportunities.
-- Getting a job as an international student will be the hardest thing you will ever go through.

3. Education loans will become a burden you can't get easily rid of.
-- Low salaries and high interest rates can make you feel lost in a pool of debt.

4. There will come days that will be the loneliest time of your lives.
-- Depression, anxiety, and loneliness are unsaid realities of the lives of some international students.

5. Life will be very unpredictable and you'll feel lost between two worlds.
-- Layoffs and visa issues make the future very unclear and it may feel like the struggle never ends.

However, on the other side life in the US will introduce you to a diverse community, a beautiful country, meaningful experiences, and a journey of self-transformation, where you get an opportunity to build a life of freedom and prosperity.

Here's my Ultimate SQL Quick Reference Guide:

(Save this post now for your interview prep)

Most candidates learn SQL by watching tutorials and copying queries.

But interviews don't test copy-paste skills.
They test fundamentals.

This cheat sheet visually breaks down everything you need:

• What SELECT, WHERE, ORDER BY, DISTINCT actually do and when to use each
• The real difference between WHERE and HAVING and why mixing them up fails your interview
• How every JOIN works - Inner, Left, Right, Full, Self, Cross -  with what gets included and excluded
• When to use a Subquery vs a CTE and why CTEs win in interviews every time
• Window Functions explained simply -  the concept that separates junior analysts from senior ones
• Indexes and performance optimization -  because writing a query and writing a fast query are two different skills
• Every constraint you need to know -  PRIMARY KEY, FOREIGN KEY, NOT NULL, UNIQUE, CHECK, AUTO_INCREMENT
• DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP -  getting this wrong in production is a very bad day
• Date and Time functions — DATEDIFF, DATE_ADD, DATE_SUB, TIMESTAMP -  every business question involves time
• OLTP vs OLAP, ACID properties, Normalization vs Denormalization -  what senior roles actually test

Remember: Daily practice is key to acing SQL interviews.

Top Platforms to Practice SQL:
-- DataLemur 🐵 (Ace the SQL & Data Interview) - https://datalemur.com/
-- StrataScratch - www.stratascratch.com/
-- LeetCode - https://leetcode.com/
-- HackerRank - https://lnkd.in/gDBqdrNP
-- Mode - https://mode.com/

Top YouTubers to Learn SQL:
1. Alex Freberg
2. Ankit Bansal
3. Luke Barousse
4. Ken Jee
5. Shashank Mishra 🇮🇳

From basic queries to advanced data modeling - this guide gives you the full picture.

Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7260322350053654528

7 Things You Should NOT Say in a Job Interview:
(And What To Say Instead, To Get An Offer)

Interviews are like first dates:
Make a good impression, and you're one step closer to landing the job (or a second date!). 

But certain phrases can make or break a good interview, ultimately wasting all your preparation and efforts. 

Here are 7 things to avoid saying in a job interview:

1. "I don't know." - It’s okay not to have all the answers, but try rephrasing in a more positive sense. 
Instead Say, - “I’ll look into that” or “I’m eager to learn.”

2. "I just need a job." - Employers want candidates who are passionate about the role. 
Instead - Highlight why you’re specifically interested in their company.

3. "What does your company do?" - Basic research is crucial. 
Instead - Convey to the interviewer that you’re serious by knowing their mission and recent projects.

4. "My last boss was terrible." - Even if it’s true, stay positive. Speaking ill of a former employer may raise red flags.
Instead - Give genuine reasons for the negative and positive aspects of the work culture.

5. "I don’t have any questions." - Not asking questions can come across as disinterest. 
Instead - Prepare thoughtful questions about the company or role, and ask them at the end of the interview.

6. I’ll do anything”- Portraying yourself as too needy makes you lose your leverage as a candidate. 
Instead say: I am passionate about [XYZ]. How can I leverage my skills to [XYZ] area in the company?

7. "I have no weaknesses." - Avoid perfectionism! It often conveys exactly the opposite of a growth mindset.
Instead - Share a genuine area for growth and how you’re addressing it recently.

Lastly, remember that every interaction is an opportunity to showcase your best self. 

Be prepared, show interest, and let your authentic self shine. 

With the right preparation, I’m sure you’ll land the right opportunity soon.

This is the Dark Reality of Studying abroad:
Many International students are anxious
Many International students are underconfident
Many International students are overwhelmed

I often hear these sentences:
“I am sorry for my accent”
“I am sorry for asking a question, I feel anxious to speak up”

Most International students are afraid to be themselves!
I was the same too, when I started my International Student journey 3 years ago.

However, I soon realized:
— I flew 8,000 miles to chase my dreams.
— I left all my family and friends behind.
— I am mastering subjects in a second language.
— I am working 12+ everyday to survive in a foreign country
Then, Why am I apologizing for being extraordinary?"

Here’s the reason:
"Because I felt I didn’t deserve to be here."

Everyone simply seems to be better than me.
The anxiety is real, The imposter syndrome is real!

International Students are underconfident and overwhelmed because they are: -- Carrying $80,000+ in loans while earning $13/hour 
-- Celebrating achievements alone while family is continents away 
-- Switching between cultures 20 times a day 
-- Living with the constant fear that one mistake ends everything
This isn’t normal people, infact, it’s almost superhuman!

Therefore, next time you feel underconfident, please remember: 
-- Your accent isn't broken English - it's evidence of your courage 
-- Your questions aren't stupid - they're proof you're growing 
-- Your different perspective isn't a problem - its a sign of wisdom 
-- Your struggle isn't a weakness - it's your superpower!

To every international student reading this:
You are not meant to go into a shell, You are meant to expand.

Start owning:
1. Your unique perspective - companies need it
2. Your resilience - it's unmatched
3. Your story - it's your greatest asset

For every international student still finding their voice - stop whispering your worth and start owning your voice.

Your dreams are valid. Your presence is powerful. Your future is limitless!

--- Post 165 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 269 reactions, 62 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,987)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7260684688137035776

Did you know that up to 70% of job openings are never advertised online? 
(It's called the “Hidden Job Market", and how you can tap into it)

The "Hidden Job Market" is where many great roles are filled through referrals, networking, and internal recommendations.

Here’s how you can tap into these hidden opportunities and uncover roles that others may never see:

1. Leverage Your Network: Reach out to former colleagues, friends, and mentors. Ask them about opportunities within their organizations or fields. Often, companies prefer to hire people recommended by trusted employees!


2. Engage on LinkedIn: Engage with content from companies or leaders in your target industry. Comment thoughtfully, share posts, and connect with recruiters. Consistent interaction can put you on their radar before a role is even posted.


3. Connect with Recruiters: Many recruiters keep a "talent pipeline" of candidates. Build connections, have informal chats, and stay in touch. When a relevant role opens up, you could be the first they think of.


4. Attend Industry Events: Webinars, conferences, and virtual meet-ups are full of hiring managers and professionals who know of internal openings. Strike up conversations, and you may learn about opportunities in advance.


5. Join Professional Groups: LinkedIn groups, alumni networks, and industry-specific forums often post job leads and insider tips. These groups are gold mines for networking and finding unadvertised roles.


6. Cold Outreach: Don’t be afraid to message hiring managers or department heads. Express interest in their team and ask to be considered for future openings. A personalized message shows initiative and can make a lasting impression.

Here are the most important networking resources to help you find your next job:

1. Meetup - www.meetup.com
2. Luma - https://lu.ma
3. Fishbowl - www.fishbowlapp.com
4. Blind - www.teamblind.com
5. Iscicle - https://iscicle.com

BONUS: Check out BuildML and Break Into Data to find network with Data Science folks.


Lastly, remember that being proactive can open doors to roles you’d never see in a job search. 

Be intentional, consistent, and genuine, and you’ll be surprised at the opportunities that come your way.

May you find the right opportunity soon.
All The Best!

I'm 28 years old, and I'm a failure.

Not because I didn't achieve my goals, But because I achieved the wrong ones:

Here’s what success looks like from the outside:
-- Having a six-figure salary
-- 120,000+ LinkedIn followers 
-- Fancy car and nice apartment 
-- Visiting new countries and experiences
-- Featured on TEDx, CNBC and Times Square

According to society: I’ve made it!

However, have you ever felt empty even after having everything?
I sure have, more now than ever before.

Expectations and comparison are the biggest thieves of happiness.

Over the past few months, this is what I have realized:
I was chasing the wrong success metrics!

Here’s what success really looks like:
-- Spending real quality time with family
-- (not just sending money back home)

-- Having a healthy mind and body
-- (not just a busy calendar throughout the week)

-- Helping people genuinely and changing lives
-- (not just posting for LinkedIn engagement)

-- Finding inner peace through spirituality
-- (not just a fancy lifestyle on Instagram)

To everyone chasing what I chased:
The salary is great - Get it. 
The title feels good - Earn it. 
The recognition is nice - Enjoy it.

But don't sacrifice your peace for it. 
Don't lose your family for it. 
Don't forget yourself in the process.

Always remember: True success is feeling content and happy, even when you don’t have the above and even when you have everything!

Be kind to yourself and recognize how far you've come.
Once you accept your true self, life becomes such a beautiful experience!

--- Post 169 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 310 reactions, 35 comments, 17 reposts (Score: 1,934)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7383503057046814721

I learned this networking strategy from a PM at Google.

Then this strategy got me an Amazon, Google & CVS Health Interview:

Today, Informational interviews are key for getting callbacks. 
Here's my step-by-step guide to get tons of Informational interviews:

𝗦𝘁𝗲𝗽 𝟭: 𝗙𝗶𝗻𝗱 𝗣𝗲𝗼𝗽𝗹𝗲 𝗪𝗵𝗼 𝗪𝗔𝗡𝗧 𝘁𝗼 𝗛𝗲𝗹𝗽
Not everyone is approachable. But here's who actually responds:

-- Find professionals who actively post on LinkedIn 
-- Approach people 3-7 years into their career (not VPs/Directors) 
-- Use LinkedIn's "People also viewed" feature for solid profiles
-- Reach out to alumni from your school (Best response rate)

𝗦𝘁𝗲𝗽 𝟮: 𝗧𝗵𝗲 "𝗪𝗮𝗿𝗺-𝗨𝗽" 𝗦𝘁𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗲𝗴𝘆
Don't cold message directly, provide value first!

Week 1: Engage with 3-4 of their posts (genuine comments) 
Week 2: Send a thoughtful connection request referencing their content/work
Week 3: Message them requesting for a 15 minute informational chat

This feels like continuing a conversation, not starting one.

𝗦𝘁𝗲𝗽 𝟯: 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗠𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝗧𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗚𝗲𝘁𝘀 𝗥𝗲𝘀𝗽𝗼𝗻𝘀𝗲𝘀
Feel free to steal my exact template:

"Hi Sohan,
Your recent post about [specific topic] really resonated - especially [specific detail they mentioned].

I'm currently [brief context: transitioning to/learning/building skills in] data analytics and have a specific question about [their experience/tool/approach].
Would you be open to a 15-minute call next week? 
Happy to work around your schedule.

Looking forward to learning from you!

Thanks, 
[Your Name]"

𝗪𝗵𝘆 𝗧𝗵𝗶𝘀 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸𝘀:
✅ References their actual content (shows you're paying attention) 
✅ ONE specific question (not "tell me everything") 
✅ Time-bound commitment (15 minutes, not open-ended)

𝗗𝘂𝗿𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗖𝗮𝗹𝗹:
-- Prepare 5-6 specific questions 
-- Take notes (shows you value their time) 
-- Ask: "Whats a major problem you're solving at work today?" 
-- End with: "Anyone else you'd recommend I talk to?"

𝗔𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗖𝗮𝗹𝗹:
Within 24 hours, send this:
"Thanks for taking the time yesterday, Sohan. 
Your insight about [specific point] was really valuable - I'm already [action you're taking].
If there's ever anything I can help with, let me know!"

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗥𝗲𝘀𝘂𝗹𝘁:
In my experience, 9 out of 10 times, they themselves will give you a referral/interview opportunity upfront!

BONUS: You can ace your next interview by preparing using Final Round AI.
Start Preparing Here: https://lnkd.in/gsCKAg7K

𝗣𝗿𝗼 𝗧𝗶𝗽: Stop networking to get something. Start networking to learn something new and the results will follow.

Here's my Ultimate Python Commands Cheatsheet for Data Analytics: 
(Save this post - you will use it more than you think)

SQL gets you in the door. 
Python gets you promoted.

Every analyst I have hired who moved up fastest had one thing in common - they did not just know Python, they knew which commands actually matter for real analytics work.

This cheatsheet covers the 20 commands you will use every single day:
-- Loading and exploring data with head(), tail(), info(), describe() 
-- Filtering and selecting with loc, iloc, query(), and conditions 
-- Aggregating with groupby(), pivot_table(), and value_counts() 
-- Combining datasets with merge() and join() 
-- Handling missing data with fillna(), dropna(), and drop() 
-- Date operations with to_datetime() and date filtering 
-- Advanced calculations with rolling(), cumsum(), and apply() 
-- Exporting clean data with to_csv()

Here is the honest truth:

You do not need to know everything. 
You need to know these commands deeply and know when to use each one.

Real Python skill is knowing which 5 commands to chain together to go from raw messy data to a clean business insight in under 30 minutes.

5 Free YouTube Resources to Learn Python for Data Analytics:
--Alex Freberg - https://lnkd.in/gJ75EQZE
--Keith Galli - youtube.com/@KeithGalli
--Corey Schafer - youtube.com/@coreyms
--freeCodeCamp - youtube.com/@freecodecamp
--Luke Barousse - youtube.com/@LukeBarousse

Save this. Build something with it. Watching videos without practicing is not learning.

Which command do you use most in your daily work?

💭 Tag someone who keeps saying they will learn Python "next month"

Master SQL Joins to ace your next interview:

Most candidates learn JOINs by memorizing patterns:
INNER JOIN, LEFT JOIN, RIGHT JOIN, FULL JOIN.

They test understanding.

From getting 500+ rejections,
To getting hired by FAANG:

This is the successful 3-Phase Application strategy of a follower of mine:

Phase 1: Target & Research
→ Applied to 5-10 companies/week max 
→ Spent 30 minutes researching each company 
→ Found hiring manager's name on LinkedIn 
→ Identified 2-3 specific company challenges

Phase 2: Customize Everything
→ Tailored resume for each role (ATS friendly) 
→ Used exact keywords from job description 
→ Created company-specific cover letters 
→ Added quantified achievements relevant to their needs

Phase 3: Strategic Follow-up 
→ Applied Tuesday-Thursday between 8-11am 
→ Connected with hiring manager within 24 hours 
→ Sent value-add message ("provide value first") 
→ Followed up once after 2 weeks with key industry insight

The journey wasn't easy, however, consistent and targeted efforts made all the difference.

Here's the breakdown of applications:
-- 500 applications sent
-- 60 responses received
-- 35 interviews scheduled
-- 8 final rounds
-- 3 offers

What To Stop Stopped Doing: 
✗ Mass applying with generic resumes 
✗ Not using Referrals and Cover Letters 
✗ Following up too aggressively

Here's What Actually Worked: 
✓ "Problem-solution" cover letter format 
✓ Portfolio/case study links in applications 
✓ Referencing recent company news/achievements
✓ Showing genuine interest in their mission

Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7445350993304924160

Here's my Ultimate Data Visualization Cheatsheet: 
(Save this - knowing which chart to use is a skill most analysts never develop)

Most analysts pick charts by habit. The best analysts pick charts by purpose.
Here is when to use each one 👇

Heat Map Use to compare large volumes of data using color gradients. 
-- Ex: Customer engagement trends across regions and products

Pareto Chart Use to rank categories by impact and apply the 80/20 rule visually. 
-- Ex: Focus on the top issues causing delays or errors

Speedometer Chart Use to show progress against a single target instantly. 
-- Ex: Monitor customer satisfaction scores on a rolling basis

Sparkline Chart Use for compact trend visuals embedded directly inside a table. 
-- Ex: Monitor revenue fluctuations across quarters inside a single cell

Box & Whisker Plot Use to reveal variability, outliers, and median across distributions. 
-- Ex: Contrast delivery times across different suppliers

Sunburst Chart Use to explore nested categories in a radial drill-down layout. 
-- Ex: Department-wise expense breakdown by subcategories

Fill-up Chart Use to represent percentage-based progress in a clean visual format. 
-- Ex: Show budget utilization or course completion rate in a report

Radar Chart Use to visualize performance across multiple metrics in one shape. 
-- Ex: Rate employee skills across different categories for reviews

Treemap Chart Use to show proportional size and groupings simultaneously. 
-- Ex: Visualize how each product contributes to total sales

Histogram Use to show frequency distribution of numerical data. 
-- Ex: Review how many customers fall into each purchase range

Scatter Plot Use to map paired numerical data and identify trends or clusters. -- Ex: Compare ad spend with sales performance to find strong matches

Funnel Chart Use to display step-by-step attrition in a linear process. 
-- Ex: Analyze newsletter signup journey from visit to confirmation

Choosing the wrong chart does not just look bad. It slows down decisions. Confuses stakeholders. Makes your analysis look weaker than it is.
Know your charts. Pick by purpose. Not by habit.
Which of these do you use most in your daily work?

♻️ Repost to help someone level up their data visualization 
💭 Tag a data analyst who needs to see this 
📩 Get my full data analytics career guide: https://lnkd.in/gjUqmQ5H

Data without a story is just noise:


Raw numbers don’t drive decisions—clear insights do.
And the best way to make data meaningful? Visualize it.

Most people stare at spreadsheets but miss the bigger picture.
Because without the right visuals, critical insights get lost.

Here’s how to turn numbers into impact:
✅ Spot the trend – Identify the key message in your data.
✅ Choose the right chart – The right visual makes all the difference.
✅ Simplify the complex – Make insights instantly clear.
✅ Tell a story – Make it stick, make it drive action.

The best analysts don’t just report data.
They craft visual stories that inspire action.

--- Post 186 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 255 reactions, 52 comments (Score: 1,795)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7392208633767329792

I am proud to share that I’ve won the Hermes Creative Award!

However, until this moment, even I couldn't believe this was happening:
Because 18 months ago, I wasn't creating content.

I was creating excuses: 
→ "My English isn't good enough" 
→ "Nobody wants to hear my story" 
→ "I'm not a real content creator" 
→ "I have nothing valuable to say"

Then something shifted.

I stopped asking "Am I good enough to post?" 
Started asking "Can this post help someone today?"

The first post I wrote: 44 views. 3 likes.
I almost thought of deleting my account.

The post that changed everything:
About my honest struggles as an International Student 250,000+ views. Hundreds of messages.

"I thought I was alone in this." 
"You just described my life." 
"Thank you for being real."

That's when I understood: 
People don't connect with perfection. They connect with truth.

What winning the Hermes Creative Award actually means:
Not that I'm a great creator. 
But that being real resonates 300 posts later: 
→ 120,000+ community built 
→ Thousands of students helped 
→ Countless lives impacted 
→ Now, internationally recognized

But here's what matters more than the award:
-- The student who didn't quit because of my post. 
-- The person who felt less alone reading my struggles. 
-- The job seeker who tried one more time after seeing my story.

That's the real win.

However, If I could do this with limited talent, you can do so much more!

To the person reading this who feels like they have nothing special to offer:
You do.

Your story. Your struggle. Your success. That's your value. 
That's your award waiting to happen soon.

No matter what you’re struggling with, believe in yourself and don’t give up.
The world will be hearing your story soon.

Keep growing, God will be kind!

============================================================

--- Post 193 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 226 reactions, 51 comments, 43 reposts (Score: 1,726)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7449473788087361538

Here are 12 indispensable data analyst skills and the exact book for each one.
These are not random recommendations.

Every single one directly made me a better analyst.

Save this list 👇

SQL
SQL for Data Analytics by Upom Malik
-- The most practical SQL book I have found for analysts specifically
-- https://lnkd.in/g3kNNNJe 

Statistics
Practical Statistics for Data Scientists by Peter Bruce
-- 50+ essential concepts explained without unnecessary math complexity
-- https://lnkd.in/gE4FxH8k 

Storytelling
Storytelling with Data by Cole Nussbaumer Knaflic
-- The book that changed how I present findings to stakeholders forever
-- https://lnkd.in/gbdaUa39 

Automation
Automate the Boring Stuff with Python by Al Sweigart
-- Completely free online. The best introduction to Python automation that exists.
-- https://lnkd.in/g236C8HJ 

A/B Testing
Trustworthy Online Controlled Experiments by Ron Kohavi
-- Written by the people who ran A/B testing at Microsoft and Google
-- https://lnkd.in/gtagQNbn 

Data Bias
Weapons of Math Destruction by Cathy O'Neil
-- Every analyst needs to understand how data can mislead and harm
-- https://lnkd.in/g__gaNd3 

Problem Solving
Data Science for Business by Foster Provost
-- The best book on how to think about business problems using data
-- https://lnkd.in/gKYbVww5 

Clarity
Information Dashboard Design by Stephen Few
-- If you build dashboards this is required reading. Period.
-- https://lnkd.in/gqgm4ipH 

Productivity
Getting Things Done by David Allen
-- The analyst who cannot manage their own workflow cannot manage data projects
-- https://lnkd.in/ggZiGks7 

Git
Git Pocket Guide by Richard Silverman
-- Version control is no longer optional for analysts in 2026
-- https://lnkd.in/gVzCNR2t 

Communication
Say It With Charts by Gene Zelazny
-- Teaches you to choose the right chart for the right message every time
-- https://lnkd.in/gPEREBTG 

Data Cleaning
Bad Data Handbook by Q. Ethan McCallum
-- 80% of analytics work is cleaning data. This is the only book dedicated to doing it right.
-- https://lnkd.in/g8fNfrZd 

Most analysts invest in tools.
The best analysts invest in knowledge.

Tools change every year.
The thinking behind good analysis never does.

Which of these have you already read?

============================================================

Over 98% Resume's get rejected by the ATS,

Follow these tips by a Google Recruiter to fix your Resume instantly:

Most resumes fail at the screening stage due to
• Over-designed templates
• Incorrect section structure
• Missing or misplaced keywords
• Unreadable formatting for ATS software

Here’s exactly how you should structure a good resume:

1. Summary:
-- Write a short 2-3 line objective to position yourself and display your value.

2. Education:
-- Let the hiring manager know the knowledge that you’ve been trained for.

3. Skills:
-- Convey the skills that you can contribute to the team.

4. Work Experience:
-- Showcase how your previous experience will help you successfully carry out the responsibilities of the role that you’re interviewing for.

5. Projects:
-- This section helps you portray your expertise in a broad range of skill sets.

6. Extra-Curricular & Leadership:
-- Portraying a holistic personality further adds to the credibility of you as a candidate.

Having your resume structured this way will be the easiest way for the hiring manager to select you as a potential candidate and send out an interview invite.

In addition, here are a few tips for a fully optimized resume:

-- If you have less than 10 years of experience, always keep a 1-page resume.
-- Make sure to include tons of metrics and data to support your achievements
-- Always format your bullet like this: Accomplished [X] as measured by [Y], by doing [Z].
-- Tailor a unique resume for each job application to make it ATS-friendly.

If you are currently struggling to get callbacks, try these suggestions.
I assure you, that by implementing them for just 2-3 weeks, you’ll see an increase in the number of callbacks that you get.

Here's the step by step roadmap I followed for my O-1 visa prep:

You can get a free 30-min consultation and evaluate your profile for free with experienced lawyers from Manifest Law.

Free Consultation Link: https://lnkd.in/gQpAmBb7

Step 2: Profile building based on lawyer guidance

You need to meet 3 out of 8 criteria to qualify for an O-1. 
Example: (1) Published Material, (2) Judging Others' Work, (3) Critical Capacity.

Months 1-4: Foundation 
→ Write 12-16 technical articles (3-4/month on IEEE, Analytics Vidhya, Hackernoon, Towards Data Science) 
→ Document work impact with metrics (users affected, revenue, performance gains) 
→ Start GitHub portfolio (contribute to 2-3 high-impact repos)

Months 5-7: Visibility & Recognition
→ Judge 3-4 hackathons (Devpost, MLH, local university events) 
→ Review pull requests on major open-source projects (document with GitHub activity) 
→ Peer review articles on platforms like arXiv, technical publications 
→ Speak at 4-5 events (2 local meetups via Meetup.com, 2-3 virtual conferences)

Months 8-9: Media & Credibility 
→ Get 4-6 media mentions in tech publications through qwoted.com
→ Pitch guest posts to TechCrunch, InfoWorld, Built In, The New Stack

Months 10-11: Assemble Critical Evidence
→ Organize all evidence by O-1 criterion in detailed folders 
→ Request 5-7 recommendation letters (managers, tech leads, conference organizers) 
→ Get 1-2 expert opinion letters from recognized authorities in your field 
→ Compile detailed itinerary of US work (current + future projects) 

Month 12: File & Execute
→ Lawyer drafts petition narrative 
→ Finalize Form I-129 with all supporting docs 
→ File with Premium Processing ($2,805 for 15-day decision)

Expected outcome: Decision in 2 weeks, approval in Month 13

Total Investment:
Time: 6-8 hours/week 
Money: Memberships ($99-198) + Legal/Filing ($10-13K in Month 12)

--- Post 199 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 313 reactions, 7 comments, 18 reposts (Score: 1,671)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7247622772498055170

This is a famous SQL Interview question asked by Google:

Question: Google's marketing team is making a Superbowl commercial and needs a simple statistic to put on their TV ad: the median number of searches a person made last year. Write a query to report the median of searches made by a user. Round the median to one decimal point.

You can find the full details here: https://lnkd.in/ggW7tQHt

Here’s how I would tackle this question:

Step 1: Understand the problem
We need to calculate the median number of searches per user based on a summary table that contains:

searches: The number of searches a user made.
num_users: How many users made that number of searches.

We can’t directly query individual user data due to scale but can use the summary data to calculate the median efficiently.

Step 2: Plan the solution
The median is the middle value in a sorted dataset. Given that we have summarized data, we can treat num_users as representing that many individual users and then find the median across these "expanded" rows.

Step 3: Expand the dataset
We simulate the data expansion by using GENERATE_SERIES (or a similar function) to create multiple rows per num_users, each representing a user’s search count.

Step 4: Calculate the median
Once the dataset is expanded, we use PERCENTILE_CONT(0.50) to find the median, which calculates the value at the 50th percentile.

This is a semi-hard problem and should take between 10-20 minutes to get to a solution.

I hope this helps you ace your next SQL Interview.

'Where do you see yourself in 5 years?'
This was the hardest question I faced in my first job interview in the USA:

I froze!

Not because I didn't have dreams, but because as an international student, I couldn't even see past the next few months:
-- My bank account was almost empty 
-- My student loans were piling up 
-- My visa status was uncertain 
-- My job search felt like an endless rejection loop

When you're surviving on 2-minute Maggi noodles and applying to 100+ jobs every week, a 5-year plan feels like a luxury you can't afford.

But here's what I wish I had known then:
Sometimes not being able to see the future is exactly what pushes you to create it.

In those moments of uncertainty: 
-- I learned to take things one day at a time 
-- I focused on small, achievable goals 
-- I celebrated tiny wins 
-- I kept showing up, even when it felt pointless

Today, 2 years later: 
-- I have a stable job at a company I love 
-- I've built a supportive community on LinkedIn 
-- I can live with much less financial stress
-- I'm planning that 5-year future I once couldn't imagine

To every international student feeling lost in the job search right now:
1. Your current situation is not your final destination
2. Those rejection emails don't define your worth
3. Your struggles today are building your strength for tomorrow

Remember: The same uncertainty that keeps you awake at night is preparing you for opportunities you can't even imagine yet.

If you're in the middle of your job search journey, keep going. 
Your breakthrough might be just one application away!

If my story resonates with you, then share your story in the comments below. 
Your experience might be exactly what someone else needs to hear today.

Keep believing, keep persisting, keep growing! 🌱

--- Post 206 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 288 reactions, 17 comments, 1 reposts (Score: 1,612)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7226241408574046211

Part 4 of 7 in my series "Life as a New International Student",
How one conversation before my visa interview changed my life: 

Back in November of 2021 when I was appearing for my US visa interview in New Delhi, I reached the embassy 20 minutes early and hence waited in line until my number was called for the interview.

While waiting, I saw that a homeless person approached a fellow applicant, asking for money. The homeless person was very adamant. The man seemed flustered, clearly uncomfortable with the situation. 

Without a second thought, I intervened, politely asking the homeless person to give him some space. This sparked a conversation that would change the course of my life.

The man introduced himself as another visa applicant. He had come for his H1-B visa while I was applying for my F-1 visa. 

As we chatted, I discovered something incredible – He had graduated from the same university that I was about to head to i.e. The University of Illinois at Chicago. The coincidence was uncanny, and it set the stage for a conversation filled with invaluable insights.

I asked him a lot of questions and by the end of it, he gave me invaluable advice, He said: Chicago is a very expensive city. The cost of living is quite high. "Get a Research Assistantship, It'll ease the financial burden significantly."

After listening to his advice, it had left a deep impact on me. I thanked him for his advice and went for my interview.

Cut to, when I landed in Chicago, and even before my classes began, I started mailing all the professors from the faculty directory and requested for assistantships. I sent out over 500 random emails, showcasing skills and how my work can make an impact on their project. However as fate would have it, I did not receive anything in the next 6 months. I was left disappointed.

Since I had taken a student loan, the financial burden was immense. For months, I juggled part-time jobs, struggling to make ends meet. But just when I was on the verge of giving up, a glimmer of hope emerged. One of those random emails I had sent months ago bore fruit. I was asked to interview and soon I landed an assistantship that not only covered my tuition but also provided a great stipend.

It was a turning point. The financial burden was lifted and it all stemmed from a chance encounter and a brief conversation.
 
I couldn't be more grateful for that 15-minute conversation. It changed my life in ways I couldn't have imagined. To anyone who is going through a period of uncertainty, anxiety, or stress, I hope this post serves as the same guiding light just like that one conversation was to me. 

Keep believing in your dreams and keep doing the work, I can assure you that the universe will conspire to surprise you and will manifest your wildest dreams into reality.

Did you have any similar experiences? Share your thoughts below!

--- Post 207 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 270 reactions, 20 comments, 15 reposts (Score: 1,580)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7351977805900890112

I spoke with a FAANG Recruiter and learned how they shortlisted candidates for interviews:

The magic to get shortlisted is not 100% your talent. It's your resume!

Section 1: The ATS Reality Check
70% of resumes die before human eyes see them.

Here's what actually works: 
-- Standard headings: "Work Experience," "Skills," "Education" 
-- No fancy formatting that is not readable by the ATS System
-- Keywords naturally woven into bullet points 
-- Clean, scannable layout

Pro tip: Build your resume using LATEX. If it's still readable, it's ATS-friendly.

Section 2: The 6-Second Recruiter Scan

Here's what recruiters actually look for:
1. Clear job title matching the role
2. Recognizable company names
3. Relevant years of experience
4. Key skills matching the job description

Pro Tip: Instead of: "Performed data analysis using Python", Write: "Developed predictive churn model using XGBoost that improved customer retention by 18%, generating $450K in annual recurring revenue"

Numbers talk. Everything else is noise.

Section 3: The Referral Hack

The truth: Almost 50% of hires come from referrals.

Here's the strategy for getting solid referrals: 
→ Reach out to professionals on LinkedIn from Target Companies
→ Keep it short & specific
→ Mention the exact role you’re applying for
→ Show company knowledge (Not just “I love Tesla” – be specific!)
→ Request a quick chat, NOT just a referral

The Bottom Line:
Your resume isn't a comprehensive work history. It's a document designed to get your foot in the door.

Make every word count → Lead with impact →  Get referred when possible.
You've mastered the hardest part (the skills). Don't let a weak resume hold you back.

BONUS: If you're struggling with crafting a ATS Friendly resume, Try Wisedoc AI Recruiting’s AI tools to optimize your profile and increase callbacks in your job search.

--- Post 208 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7230194812224741376

Here’s my no B.S guide to Networking on LinkedIn:

I replied to 500+ messages last week, most of them were requests for referrals.
Here’s some of the real messages I got:

— “Great connecting with you, how have you been?”
— “I hope you are doing well, can we connect?”
— ‘’I am desperate, can you provide me a referral?”
— “I see that we both know XYZ person, want to connect?”
— ‘’I know you are reading this, can you give me a referral bro?”

Okay, you got the picture. Even though I reply to all my messages, there’s a high chance your potential referral will not reply to these.

In a highly saturated job market, taking the time and asking for referrals is already a big task.
Why make it even more hard for yourself?

Here’s a template that can make your life easy:

“Hello XYZ,
I am Sohan, an aspiring business analyst with 3+ years of experience and a recent graduate from UIC. I am interested in the Senior Business Analyst Role at Microsoft.

Here is the Job ID: #123453

It would be immensely helpful if you could refer me for this position.
Please find my resume and other details attached below. I am grateful for your support.

Best Regards,
Sohan”

If you send a message to just 10 people in your dream company, I am sure at least one will respond. That’s worth a shot!

There’s a whole world of opportunities out there waiting for you to reach out to them.
Try this approach and let me know if it works for you!

What are your thoughts on getting referrals? Let me know in the comments section.

- Record each interview, learn from your flaws, and do better the next time.
- Lastly, Apply to roles as much as POSSIBLE (Without compromising Quality)

Most Important: Every 2 weeks; Self-evaluate, acknowledge that you are doing everything that you possibly can, be proud of your efforts, and practice GRATITUDE.

Consistency and a Laser-Focus is how you blow past everyone else!
Do you agree?

============================================================

--- Post 219 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 244 reactions, 24 comments, 32 reposts (Score: 1,524)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7411408349881368576

I am hiring you as a Data Analyst in 2026:

(If you can answer these 30 SQL questions)

A. WINDOWS FUNCTIONS:
1. Calculate the cumulative sum of sales for each region.
2. Rank products by their sales within each category.
3. Determine a running total of expenses by transaction date.
4. Identify the top 5 performing sales representatives by quarter.
5. Compute the year-over-year difference in revenue for each store.

B. COMMON TABLE EXPRESSIONS(CTE):
1. Write a recursive CTE to calculate Fibonacci numbers.
2. Use a CTE to identify the reporting hierarchy for a given employee.
3. Flatten a tree-structured data model using a CTE.
4. Use a recursive CTE to find the path between two nodes in a graph.
5. Analyze monthly sales trends using CTEs for better readability.

C. JOINS (INNER, OUTER, CROSS, SELF):
1. List customers who have purchased a specific product (Inner Join).
2. Identify orders that have no assigned delivery agent (Left Join).
3. Get products that were never sold (Right Join).
4. Generate all possible combinations of stores and their products (Cross Join).
5. Pair employees within the same team for mentorship opportunities (Self Join).

D. SUBQUERIES:
1. Find products priced above the average price in their category.
2. Identify employees who earn more than the average salary of their peers.
3. List customers who placed orders in every month of the year.
4. Retrieve the second and third highest salaries using subqueries.
5. Determine regions where no orders have been placed.

E. AGGREGATE FUNCTIONS:
1. Calculate the total sales for each product category.
2. Find the average number of orders per customer.
3. Identify the maximum, minimum, and average revenue generated by stores.
4. Count the number of active customers in each region.
5. Compute the variance and standard deviation of product prices.

F. INDEXING AND QUERY OPTIMIZATION:
1. Identify slow-running queries that could benefit from indexing.
2. Analyze the execution plan to optimize performance.
3. Compare query performance before and after adding an index.
4. Write a query to find unused indexes in a database.
5. Optimize a complex join query for faster execution.

These are the 30 most commonly asked SQL questions in Technical Interviews.
So begin by practicing them now!

--- Post 221 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 261 reactions, 18 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 1,503)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7257407983695872001

This is a famous interview question from Uber :

Question: Write a query to obtain the third transaction of every user. Output the user id, spend and transaction date.

You can find full details here:
https://lnkd.in/d7b3Pmcp

Here’s my Step-by-Step Approach:

Step 1 - Understand the Requirements:

We need to retrieve the third transaction made by each user.

The output should include only the user ID, the spend amount, and the transaction date of each user’s third transaction.

Step 2 - Identify the Key Steps:

Use the ROW_NUMBER() window function to assign a unique sequential number to each transaction for every user, ordered by the transaction_date.

Filter the results to get only the rows where the transaction rank equals 3 (i.e., the third transaction for each user).

Step 3 - Write the Query:

Use a Common Table Expression (CTE) to rank each transaction per user.

Select the required columns with a filter on the row number for the third transaction.

This is a semi-medium level problem and should take about 5-10 minutes to get to the solution.

I hope this helps ace your next SQL interview.

--- Post 223 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 205 reactions, 42 comments, 24 reposts (Score: 1,493)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7442697942383357952

If I had to restart my data analytics career from scratch in 2026 -
Here is the exact 6-month roadmap I would follow.

Not based on what looks good on a resume.
Based on what I actually test candidates on when I interview them.

Month 1 - Excel & SQL Basics
Excel first. Pivot tables, VLOOKUP, basic formulas. Understand how data is structured before you touch a database.
Then SQL. SELECT, WHERE, GROUP BY, JOIN. Do not move forward until you can write a query without Googling the syntax.

Practice platforms: DataLemur, HackerRank

Month 2 - Intermediate SQL + Data Cleaning
Window functions, CTEs, subqueries. These are what I test in every single interview. If you cannot write ROW_NUMBER() or RANK() confidently you are not ready for a mid-level role.
Spend equal time on data cleaning. Handling nulls, duplicates, outliers. 80% of a real analyst's job lives here. Almost no tutorial covers it enough.

Month 3 - Data Visualization
Pick one tool. Power BI or Tableau. Learn it deeply before touching the other.
Build dashboards from real messy datasets — not tutorial data. The goal is not beautiful charts. The goal is answering a business question in a way a non-technical person understands immediately.

Month 4 - Python + AI Tools
Pandas and NumPy for data analysis. One end-to-end project on GitHub.
But here is what I would do differently in 2026:
Learn AI tools in parallel. Use Claude to pressure test your analysis. Use it to draft executive summaries you then edit and own. Use it to explain complex findings in plain language.

The analysts getting hired now are not just writing Python.
They are combining Python with AI to produce output in half the time.

Month 5 - Business Analytics + Storytelling
KPIs, revenue analysis, customer segmentation, churn. Study how real businesses use data.
Then practice communicating findings to someone who does not know what SQL is. If they understand it - you are ready.

This skill separates a $70K analyst from a $120K one. Not the technical stack.

Month 6 - Portfolio + Job Search
Build 3 projects:
-- A sales performance dashboard
-- A customer churn analysis
-- An operational efficiency report

Each needs a clear business question, clean code on GitHub, and one paragraph on what the business should do with the finding.

Then optimize your resume, LinkedIn, and start applying.

6 YouTube Channels to Learn Everything :
- Alex The Analyst→ https://lnkd.in/gJ75EQZE
- Luke Barousse→ youtube.com/@LukeBarousse
- Kenji Explains→ youtube.com/@KenjiExplains
- Mo Chen→ youtube.com/@mo-chen
- StatQuest with Josh Starmer→ youtube.com/@statquest
- Thu Vu Data Analytics→ youtube.com/@Thuvu5

6 months is enough if you treat it like a job.
2 hours on weekends will take you 2 years.

The roadmap is not the hard part.
The discipline is.

Where are you on this roadmap right now?

♻️ Repost to help someone just starting out
💭 Tag someone breaking into data analytics
📩 Get my full data analytics career guide: https://lnkd.in/gjUqmQ5H

--- Post 226 ---
Author: sohansethi
Date: Apr 17, 2026
Engagement: 181 reactions, 50 comments, 28 reposts (Score: 1,461)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7449202785679601664

You have been learning Python for months. 
But can you load a messy CSV and tell me what the business should do next? If not - you are learning the wrong things.

I have seen candidates spend months learning algorithms and data structures - then freeze when I ask them to load a CSV and answer a basic business question from it.

That is not a Python problem. That is a direction problem.

Here is the exact Python roadmap for data analysts, from someone who interviews them:

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟭 - 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗕𝗮𝘀𝗶𝗰𝘀
Variables, data types, loops, conditionals, and functions.
Do not spend more than 2 weeks here.
Resource: CS50P by Harvard - free at cs50.harvard.edu/python

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟮 - 𝗣𝗮𝗻𝗱𝗮𝘀 & 𝗡𝘂𝗺𝗣𝘆
This is where data analyst Python actually starts.
-- Load data with pd.read_csv()
-- Explore with head(), info(), describe()
-- Clean with fillna(), dropna(), drop()
-- Summarize with groupby(), pivot_table(), value_counts()
-- Combine with merge() and join()

If you cannot do this on a messy dataset without Googling - you are not ready for an interview.

Resource: Kaggle Learn - free at kaggle.com/learn

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟯 - 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗖𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 & 𝗘𝗗𝗔
This is what most of a real analyst's job looks like.
Handle missing values with context. Remove duplicates. Detect outliers. Convert data types. Explore distributions and trends.

Clean data is the foundation of every insight.

Resource: Keith Galli - youtube.com/@KeithGalli

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟰 - 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗩𝗶𝘀𝘂𝗮𝗹𝗶𝘇𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻
-- Matplotlib for basic charts
-- Seaborn for statistical visuals
-- Plotly for dashboards

Can you take messy data and create a visualization that answers a business question - without being told which chart to use?

That judgment is the skill.

Resource: freeCodeCamp - https://lnkd.in/gvKw8x2W

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟱 - 𝗔𝗱𝘃𝗮𝗻𝗰𝗲𝗱 𝗔𝗻𝗮𝗹𝘆𝘁𝗶𝗰𝘀
-- rolling() and cumsum() for time series
-- apply() and lambda for logic
SQL + Python together. Automate reports.

This is what gets you promoted.

𝗦𝘁𝗮𝗴𝗲 𝟲 - 𝗔𝗜 + 𝗣𝘆𝘁𝗵𝗼𝗻
-- Use Claude to pressure test your analysis
-- Use it to draft summaries
-- Use GitHub Copilot to speed up code

Python without AI in 2026 is like knowing SQL but refusing to use indexes.

You do not need to know all of Python.
You need to know the 20% that does 80% of the work - deeply.

The candidates I hire are not the ones who learned the most.
They are the ones who can clean, analyze, visualize, and explain what the business should do.

That is the roadmap. Everything else is noise.

Where are you on this right now?

♻️ Repost to help someone learning Python for data analytics
💭 Tag someone learning Python without direction
📩 Get my full data analytics career guide: https://lnkd.in/gjUqmQ5H

============================================================

Today is my 29th Birthday:

Here's a heartfelt note to my 19-year-old self 💌

At 19, I thought I was done. 

-- I only managed to get into a Tier 3 engineering college
-- I'd given up on my dream of studying abroad
-- I felt scared and insecure to the core
-- Thought I’ll live an average life at best

If I could sit with that kid today, here's what I'd say:

That feeling of being fearful about the future? It never fully goes away.
However kid: Your life will often times feel MAGICAL and not at all mediocre.

At 29, I look back with profound gratitude. 
Not despite the hardships, but majorly because of them. 

✅ Speaking on a TEDx stage

✅ Being featured in major News & Media across the world

✅ My face on a freaking Times Square Billboard

✅ Experiencing spiritual and personal growth

✅ Impacting millions of people through my words 

If this isn’t Magic, I don’t know what is (Especially for a scared 19 year-old kid)

As you read this, I want you think about your 10 year younger self too.

Look at how far you’ve come, and most importantly, look at how far you are meant to go!

Your current chapter is not your final story:

-- Your uncertainty
-- Your self-doubt
-- Your closed doors

They're not blocking your path. They're building the foundation for something bigger than you can see right now.

Trust your process and your journey will follow!

Thank you for being a part of my journey, I am grateful to be a part of yours!

I am already writing a note to my 39 year old self today, I hope that guy will change the world one day.

See you all in 10 years, God will be kind!

I have only 100 days left until my STEM OPT expires.

The worst part is, I know I am not alone.
There are thousands of lives dependent on the H1-B lottery next week!

I've spent years of hard work building my life in the USA,
However the past 2 years, it was heartbreaking to read: "Unfortunately, you were not selected in the H1-B lottery."

I am not leaving my future to luck anymore.
I want to officially announce that I am going for the O-1 visa as an alternative to the H1-B.

I'm currently working on building my case.
And I'm documenting everything publicly.

The roadmap. The resources. The wins. The rejections. My real story.

Because if you're reading this, you probably know exactly how I feel right now.
The uncertainty and The fear. You're not alone.

I'll be sharing:
→ My exact step-by-step O-1 process 
→ Free resources that actually worked for me 
→ Templates and strategies I'm currently using 
→ Conversations with immigration lawyers 
→ Every single thing I learn, I'll be sharing publicly for free.

Not because I've figured it out. Because I'm figuring it out.
And if my journey helps even one person avoid this uncertainty, it's worth it.

To everyone waiting for lottery results in a week:
I hope we all get selected. I really do.

But hope isn't a strategy.
Let's build our backup plan together.

Manifest Law is helping me with my O-1 visa and they are offering free consultations to my network.

More updates coming very soon.

Author: sohansethi
